People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s not that anything is really urgent, and everyone else is still seated, so I do notice it afterward and feel a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're seated and get the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I suppose I try to make it look less obvious. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Last Question]\nAre these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"It has started to affect my relationships at work and in my community because people lose patience with me in meetings and book club, and I leave feeling embarrassed instead of connected.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. But I do think it’s started to affect things at work and in my community.

I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. But I do think it’s started to affect things at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, I can tell sometimes people lose patience with me if I jump in or go on too long. And then I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things awkward instead of feeling connected to people. I suppose that is a problem, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy social gatherings or work-related activities because of these behaviors and feelings of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I still go to things, and I do enjoy people, but I’m more aware now that in meetings or book club I can leave feeling embarrassed if I’ve jumped in too much or people seem impatient with me.\n\nSo instead of coming away feeling connected, I sometimes come away worrying I’ve annoyed everyone. That does take some of the pleasure out of it, I suppose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Usually I don’t even notice at first, and then I think, “Right, settle down.” Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, sometimes. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. But I do think it’s started to affect things at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, I can tell sometimes people lose patience with me if I jump in or go on too long. And then I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things awkward instead of feeling connected to people. I suppose that is a problem, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy social gatherings or work-related activities because of these behaviors and feelings of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I still go to things, and I do enjoy people, but I’m more aware now that in meetings or book club I can leave feeling embarrassed if I’ve jumped in too much or people seem impatient with me.\n\nSo instead of coming away feeling connected, I sometimes come away worrying I’ve annoyed everyone. That does take some of the pleasure out of it, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these behaviors and feelings of restlessness are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d say mildly. It hasn’t stopped me doing my job or anything like that, but it has started to affect things with people at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, and at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I go home feeling embarrassed rather than feeling like I’ve connected with them. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s not anything terribly dramatic, but I’ve noticed I have a hard time just staying seated when I’m meant to. In meetings, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room… and it isn’t always really necessary. It’s just something I seem to do, and I thought perhaps I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up and move around, or does it feel more like you can't help it?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m choosing it, because I usually make it look purposeful, you know — refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. But honestly, it feels more like I get this urge to be up, and then I find a reasonable excuse for it. It’s not that anything is really urgent, and everyone else is still seated, so I do notice it afterward and feel a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're seated and get the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I suppose I try to make it look less obvious. If I’m in a meeting or even at dinner and everyone else is seated, I’ll suddenly feel like I need to get up — refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, that sort of thing. It’s not really urgent most of the time, but I feel unsettled just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I can usually follow instructions and I do try to be quite conscientious about details, especially with work. Of course, I might miss something now and then, but nothing that’s really stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s dramatic or anything, but when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in my seat. Usually I don’t even notice at first, and then I think, “Right, settle down.” Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, sometimes. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. And then I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things awkward instead of feeling connected to people. I suppose that is a problem, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy social gatherings or work-related activities because of these behaviors and feelings of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I still go to things, and I do enjoy people, but I’m more aware now that in meetings or book club I can leave feeling embarrassed if I’ve jumped in too much or people seem impatient with me.\n\nSo instead of coming away feeling connected, I sometimes come away worrying I’ve annoyed everyone. That does take some of the pleasure out of it, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these behaviors and feelings of restlessness are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d say mildly. It hasn’t stopped me doing my job or anything like that, but it has started to affect things with people at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, and at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I go home feeling embarrassed rather than feeling like I’ve connected with them. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: A bit distressed, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’m falling apart. It’s more that I feel upset afterward, especially when I can tell people have lost patience with me in meetings or at book club.\n\nI leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things harder or awkward, instead of feeling connected to people. So yes, it does weigh on me somewhat. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s not anything terribly dramatic, but I’ve noticed I have a hard time just staying seated when I’m meant to. In meetings, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room… and it isn’t always really necessary. It’s just something I seem to do, and I thought perhaps I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up and move around, or does it feel more like you can't help it?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m choosing it, because I usually make it look purposeful, you know — refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. But honestly, it feels more like I get this urge to be up, and then I find a reasonable excuse for it. It’s not that anything is really urgent, and everyone else is still seated, so I do notice it afterward and feel a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're seated and get the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I suppose I try to make it look less obvious. If I’m in a meeting or even at dinner and everyone else is seated, I’ll suddenly feel like I need to get up — refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, that sort of thing. It’s not really urgent most of the time, but I feel unsettled just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I can usually follow instructions and I do try to be quite conscientious about details, especially with work. Of course, I might miss something now and then, but nothing that’s really stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s dramatic or anything, but when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in my seat. Usually I don’t even notice at first, and then I think, “Right, settle down.” Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, sometimes. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. But I do think it’s started to affect things at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, I can tell sometimes people lose patience with me if I jump in or go on too long. And then I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things awkward instead of feeling connected to people. I suppose that is a problem, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy social gatherings or work-related activities because of these behaviors and feelings of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I still go to things, and I do enjoy people, but I’m more aware now that in meetings or book club I can leave feeling embarrassed if I’ve jumped in too much or people seem impatient with me.\n\nSo instead of coming away feeling connected, I sometimes come away worrying I’ve annoyed everyone. That does take some of the pleasure out of it, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these behaviors and feelings of restlessness are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d say mildly. It hasn’t stopped me doing my job or anything like that, but it has started to affect things with people at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, and at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I go home feeling embarrassed rather than feeling like I’ve connected with them. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: A bit distressed, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’m falling apart. It’s more that I feel upset afterward, especially when I can tell people have lost patience with me in meetings or at book club.\n\nI leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things harder or awkward, instead of feeling connected to people. So yes, it does weigh on me somewhat. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as difficulties with work or relationships, that you think might be related to these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… not hugely, but enough that I’ve noticed it. It’s mostly with relationships at work and in the community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, people sometimes seem to lose patience with me, and then I come away feeling embarrassed, like I’ve not handled myself very well. Instead of feeling connected to people, I end up worrying I’ve annoyed them. So yes, I suppose that is affecting my day-to-day life a bit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s not anything terribly dramatic, but I’ve noticed I have a hard time just staying seated when I’m meant to. In meetings, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room… and it isn’t always really necessary. It’s just something I seem to do, and I thought perhaps I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up and move around, or does it feel more like you can't help it?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m choosing it, because I usually make it look purposeful, you know — refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. But honestly, it feels more like I get this urge to be up, and then I find a reasonable excuse for it. It’s not that anything is really urgent, and everyone else is still seated, so I do notice it afterward and feel a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're seated and get the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I suppose I try to make it look less obvious. If I’m in a meeting or even at dinner and everyone else is seated, I’ll suddenly feel like I need to get up — refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, that sort of thing. It’s not really urgent most of the time, but I feel unsettled just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I can usually follow instructions and I do try to be quite conscientious about details, especially with work. Of course, I might miss something now and then, but nothing that’s really stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s dramatic or anything, but when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in my seat. Usually I don’t even notice at first, and then I think, “Right, settle down.” Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, sometimes. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. But I do think it’s started to affect things at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, I can tell sometimes people lose patience with me if I jump in or go on too long. And then I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things awkward instead of feeling connected to people. I suppose that is a problem, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy social gatherings or work-related activities because of these behaviors and feelings of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. That does take some of the pleasure out of it, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these behaviors and feelings of restlessness are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d say mildly. It hasn’t stopped me doing my job or anything like that, but it has started to affect things with people at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, and at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I go home feeling embarrassed rather than feeling like I’ve connected with them. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: A bit distressed, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’m falling apart. It’s more that I feel upset afterward, especially when I can tell people have lost patience with me in meetings or at book club.\n\nI leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things harder or awkward, instead of feeling connected to people. So yes, it does weigh on me somewhat. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as difficulties with work or relationships, that you think might be related to these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… not hugely, but enough that I’ve noticed it. Instead of feeling connected to people, I end up worrying I’ve annoyed them. I suppose everyone’s mind wanders sometimes, doesn’t it? But I’m not sure I’d describe it as often having trouble sustaining focus. Yes… I think I probably was, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was a terribly naughty child or anything.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s not anything terribly dramatic, but I’ve noticed I have a hard time just staying seated when I’m meant to. In meetings, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room… and it isn’t always really necessary. It’s just something I seem to do, and I thought perhaps I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up and move around, or does it feel more like you can't help it?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m choosing it, because I usually make it look purposeful, you know — refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. But honestly, it feels more like I get this urge to be up, and then I find a reasonable excuse for it. It’s not that anything is really urgent, and everyone else is still seated, so I do notice it afterward and feel a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're seated and get the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I suppose I try to make it look less obvious. If I’m in a meeting or even at dinner and everyone else is seated, I’ll suddenly feel like I need to get up — refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, that sort of thing. It’s not really urgent most of the time, but I feel unsettled just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I can usually follow instructions and I do try to be quite conscientious about details, especially with work. Of course, I might miss something now and then, but nothing that’s really stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s dramatic or anything, but when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in my seat. Usually I don’t even notice at first, and then I think, “Right, settle down.” Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, sometimes. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. But I do think it’s started to affect things at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, I can tell sometimes people lose patience with me if I jump in or go on too long. And then I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things awkward instead of feeling connected to people. I suppose that is a problem, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy social gatherings or work-related activities because of these behaviors and feelings of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I still go to things, and I do enjoy people, but I’m more aware now that in meetings or book club I can leave feeling embarrassed if I’ve jumped in too much or people seem impatient with me.\n\nSo instead of coming away feeling connected, I sometimes come away worrying I’ve annoyed everyone. That does take some of the pleasure out of it, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these behaviors and feelings of restlessness are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d say mildly. It hasn’t stopped me doing my job or anything like that, but it has started to affect things with people at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, and at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I go home feeling embarrassed rather than feeling like I’ve connected with them. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: A bit distressed, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’m falling apart. It’s more that I feel upset afterward, especially when I can tell people have lost patience with me in meetings or at book club.\n\nI leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things harder or awkward, instead of feeling connected to people. So yes, it does weigh on me somewhat. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as difficulties with work or relationships, that you think might be related to these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… not hugely, but enough that I’ve noticed it. It’s mostly with relationships at work and in the community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, people sometimes seem to lose patience with me, and then I come away feeling embarrassed, like I’ve not handled myself very well. Instead of feeling connected to people, I end up worrying I’ve annoyed them. So yes, I suppose that is affecting my day-to-day life a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a major problem with focus, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, I can usually get through tasks, especially if they’re important. I suppose everyone’s mind wanders sometimes, doesn’t it? But I’m not sure I’d describe it as often having trouble sustaining focus. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I probably was, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was a terribly naughty child or anything.\n\nI was considered bright, but my report cards did say things like I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a little path between my desk and the pencil sharpener because I was up and down so much.\n\nSo yes, compared with other children, I suppose I was more restless. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say I’ve been completely unable to work or socialise, no… that sounds a bit stronger than it feels.

But it has started to get in the way a little. At work, in meetings, and in things like book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then afterwards I feel quite embarrassed. I go home thinking, “Oh dear, I’ve done it again,” instead of feeling like I’ve enjoyed being with people.

So I’m still doing those things, but it does affect how comfortable I feel in them, if that makes sense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s not anything terribly dramatic, but I’ve noticed I have a hard time just staying seated when I’m meant to. In meetings, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room… and it isn’t always really necessary. It’s just something I seem to do, and I thought perhaps I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up and move around, or does it feel more like you can't help it?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m choosing it, because I usually make it look purposeful, you know — refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. But honestly, it feels more like I get this urge to be up, and then I find a reasonable excuse for it. It’s not that anything is really urgent, and everyone else is still seated, so I do notice it afterward and feel a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're seated and get the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I suppose I try to make it look less obvious. If I’m in a meeting or even at dinner and everyone else is seated, I’ll suddenly feel like I need to get up — refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, that sort of thing. It’s not really urgent most of the time, but I feel unsettled just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I can usually follow instructions and I do try to be quite conscientious about details, especially with work. Of course, I might miss something now and then, but nothing that’s really stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s dramatic or anything, but when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in my seat. Usually I don’t even notice at first, and then I think, “Right, settle down.” Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, sometimes. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. But I do think it’s started to affect things at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, I can tell sometimes people lose patience with me if I jump in or go on too long. And then I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things awkward instead of feeling connected to people. I suppose that is a problem, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy social gatherings or work-related activities because of these behaviors and feelings of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I still go to things, and I do enjoy people, but I’m more aware now that in meetings or book club I can leave feeling embarrassed if I’ve jumped in too much or people seem impatient with me.\n\nSo instead of coming away feeling connected, I sometimes come away worrying I’ve annoyed everyone. That does take some of the pleasure out of it, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these behaviors and feelings of restlessness are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d say mildly. It hasn’t stopped me doing my job or anything like that, but it has started to affect things with people at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, and at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I go home feeling embarrassed rather than feeling like I’ve connected with them. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: A bit distressed, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’m falling apart. It’s more that I feel upset afterward, especially when I can tell people have lost patience with me in meetings or at book club.\n\nI leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things harder or awkward, instead of feeling connected to people. So yes, it does weigh on me somewhat. In meetings, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room… and it isn’t always really necessary. It’s just something I seem to do, and I thought perhaps I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up and move around, or does it feel more like you can't help it?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m choosing it, because I usually make it look purposeful, you know — refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. But honestly, it feels more like I get this urge to be up, and then I find a reasonable excuse for it. It’s not that anything is really urgent, and everyone else is still seated, so I do notice it afterward and feel a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're seated and get the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I suppose I try to make it look less obvious. If I’m in a meeting or even at dinner and everyone else is seated, I’ll suddenly feel like I need to get up — refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, that sort of thing. It’s not really urgent most of the time, but I feel unsettled just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I can usually follow instructions and I do try to be quite conscientious about details, especially with work. Of course, I might miss something now and then, but nothing that’s really stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s dramatic or anything, but when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in my seat. Usually I don’t even notice at first, and then I think, “Right, settle down.” Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, sometimes. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: A bit distressed, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’m falling apart. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as difficulties with work or relationships, that you think might be related to these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… not hugely, but enough that I’ve noticed it. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or participate in social activities because of your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve been completely unable to work or socialise, no… that sounds a bit stronger than it feels.\n\nBut it has started to get in the way a little. I go home thinking, “Oh dear, I’ve done it again,” instead of feeling like I’ve enjoyed being with people.\n\nSo I’m still doing those things, but it does affect how comfortable I feel in them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors for most of your life, including when you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though I suppose I’ve always thought of it as just being “lively” rather than a problem.\n\nEven when I was a girl, my school reports said I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. No, I don’t think I’ve ever had to actually quit a job or end a relationship because of it. That sounds rather dramatic, and I don’t think it’s gone that far.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s not anything terribly dramatic, but I’ve noticed I have a hard time just staying seated when I’m meant to. In meetings, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room… and it isn’t always really necessary. It’s just something I seem to do, and I thought perhaps I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up and move around, or does it feel more like you can't help it?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m choosing it, because I usually make it look purposeful, you know — refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. But honestly, it feels more like I get this urge to be up, and then I find a reasonable excuse for it. It’s not that anything is really urgent, and everyone else is still seated, so I do notice it afterward and feel a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're seated and get the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I suppose I try to make it look less obvious. If I’m in a meeting or even at dinner and everyone else is seated, I’ll suddenly feel like I need to get up — refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, that sort of thing. It’s not really urgent most of the time, but I feel unsettled just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I can usually follow instructions and I do try to be quite conscientious about details, especially with work. Of course, I might miss something now and then, but nothing that’s really stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s dramatic or anything, but when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in my seat. Usually I don’t even notice at first, and then I think, “Right, settle down.” Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, sometimes. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. But I do think it’s started to affect things at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, I can tell sometimes people lose patience with me if I jump in or go on too long. And then I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things awkward instead of feeling connected to people. I suppose that is a problem, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy social gatherings or work-related activities because of these behaviors and feelings of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I still go to things, and I do enjoy people, but I’m more aware now that in meetings or book club I can leave feeling embarrassed if I’ve jumped in too much or people seem impatient with me.\n\nSo instead of coming away feeling connected, I sometimes come away worrying I’ve annoyed everyone. That does take some of the pleasure out of it, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these behaviors and feelings of restlessness are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d say mildly. It hasn’t stopped me doing my job or anything like that, but it has started to affect things with people at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, and at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I go home feeling embarrassed rather than feeling like I’ve connected with them. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: A bit distressed, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’m falling apart. It’s more that I feel upset afterward, especially when I can tell people have lost patience with me in meetings or at book club.\n\nI leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things harder or awkward, instead of feeling connected to people. So yes, it does weigh on me somewhat. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as difficulties with work or relationships, that you think might be related to these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… not hugely, but enough that I’ve noticed it. It’s mostly with relationships at work and in the community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, people sometimes seem to lose patience with me, and then I come away feeling embarrassed, like I’ve not handled myself very well. Instead of feeling connected to people, I end up worrying I’ve annoyed them. So yes, I suppose that is affecting my day-to-day life a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a major problem with focus, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, I can usually get through tasks, especially if they’re important. I suppose everyone’s mind wanders sometimes, doesn’t it? But I’m not sure I’d describe it as often having trouble sustaining focus. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I probably was, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was a terribly naughty child or anything.\n\nI was considered bright, but my report cards did say things like I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose “significantly” makes me hesitate a bit, because I’m still managing day to day. I’m going to work, I’m seeing people, I’m doing my usual things.

But it has affected things, yes. Mostly in how I get on with people at work and in my community. In meetings, or at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I come away feeling embarrassed rather than connected to them.

So perhaps it’s not stopping me functioning, exactly, but it is starting to get in the way. Is that a fair way to put it?

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s not anything terribly dramatic, but I’ve noticed I have a hard time just staying seated when I’m meant to. In meetings, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee or check a handout, or walk to the back of the room… and it isn’t always really necessary. It’s just something I seem to do, and I thought perhaps I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're choosing to get up and move around, or does it feel more like you can't help it?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say I’m choosing it, because I usually make it look purposeful, you know — refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room. But honestly, it feels more like I get this urge to be up, and then I find a reasonable excuse for it. It’s not that anything is really urgent, and everyone else is still seated, so I do notice it afterward and feel a bit silly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're seated and get the urge to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I suppose I try to make it look less obvious. If I’m in a meeting or even at dinner and everyone else is seated, I’ll suddenly feel like I need to get up — refill my coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room, that sort of thing. It’s not really urgent most of the time, but I feel unsettled just sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I can usually follow instructions and I do try to be quite conscientious about details, especially with work. Of course, I might miss something now and then, but nothing that’s really stood out to me as a pattern. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain movements or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I wouldn’t say it’s dramatic or anything, but when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, clicking a pen, shifting around in my seat. Usually I don’t even notice at first, and then I think, “Right, settle down.” Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, sometimes. I don’t mean to dominate, but in the Spanish department or at the cultural center I can sort of get carried away and give three examples when one would have been enough. Then I notice people starting to wrap things up, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I try to rein it in, but yes, I suppose I can talk more than I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I do anything terribly reckless, but… yes, in conversation I can be a bit quick off the mark. In book club, or curriculum meetings, if an answer pops into my head I sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. I don’t mean to be rude—I usually realize right after and feel badly about it. I’m trying to wait my turn more. Is that the kind of impulsive thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I have the usual moments where I walk into a room and forget why, or I have to jot something down so I don’t lose track of it, but I don’t think I’m missing appointments or forgetting important tasks in any regular way. I do try to keep lists and a calendar, which helps. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are these behaviors and feelings of restlessness causing you any problems at work or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to overstate it, because I’m still functioning and people are generally kind. But I do think it’s started to affect things at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, I can tell sometimes people lose patience with me if I jump in or go on too long. And then I leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things awkward instead of feeling connected to people. I suppose that is a problem, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy social gatherings or work-related activities because of these behaviors and feelings of restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is. I still go to things, and I do enjoy people, but I’m more aware now that in meetings or book club I can leave feeling embarrassed if I’ve jumped in too much or people seem impatient with me.\n\nSo instead of coming away feeling connected, I sometimes come away worrying I’ve annoyed everyone. That does take some of the pleasure out of it, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these behaviors and feelings of restlessness are affecting your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I’d say mildly. It hasn’t stopped me doing my job or anything like that, but it has started to affect things with people at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, and at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I go home feeling embarrassed rather than feeling like I’ve connected with them. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: A bit distressed, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’m falling apart. It’s more that I feel upset afterward, especially when I can tell people have lost patience with me in meetings or at book club.\n\nI leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things harder or awkward, instead of feeling connected to people. So yes, it does weigh on me somewhat. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as difficulties with work or relationships, that you think might be related to these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… not hugely, but enough that I’ve noticed it. It’s mostly with relationships at work and in the community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, people sometimes seem to lose patience with me, and then I come away feeling embarrassed, like I’ve not handled myself very well. Instead of feeling connected to people, I end up worrying I’ve annoyed them. So yes, I suppose that is affecting my day-to-day life a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a major problem with focus, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, I can usually get through tasks, especially if they’re important. I suppose everyone’s mind wanders sometimes, doesn’t it? But I’m not sure I’d describe it as often having trouble sustaining focus. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I probably was, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was a terribly naughty child or anything.\n\nI was considered bright, but my report cards did say things like I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a little path between my desk and the pencil sharpener because I was up and down so much.\n\nSo yes, compared with other children, I suppose I was more restless. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or participate in social activities because of your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve been completely unable to work or socialise, no… that sounds a bit stronger than it feels.\n\nBut it has started to get in the way a little. At work, in meetings, and in things like book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then afterwards I feel quite embarrassed. I go home thinking, “Oh dear, I’ve done it again,” instead of feeling like I’ve enjoyed being with people.\n\nSo I’m still doing those things, but it does affect how comfortable I feel in them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors for most of your life, including when you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though I suppose I’ve always thought of it as just being “lively” rather than a problem.\n\nEven when I was a girl, my school reports said I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that before I was ten I’d worn a little path between my desk and the pencil sharpener because I was up and down so much.\n\nSo yes, it does seem to go back a long way. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job or end a relationship because of your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had to actually quit a job or end a relationship because of it. That sounds rather dramatic, and I don’t think it’s gone that far.\n\nIt’s more that it can strain things a bit. At work, in meetings, and in my book club, I can sense people getting a little impatient with me sometimes, and then I leave feeling embarrassed rather than feeling connected to them. But I’m still there, still doing those things… if that answers the question properly.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose “significantly” makes me hesitate a bit, because I’m still managing day to day. I’m going to work, I’m seeing people, I’m doing my usual things.\n\nBut it has affected things, yes. Mostly in how I get on with people at work and in my community. In meetings, or at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I come away feeling embarrassed rather than connected to them.\n\nSo perhaps it’s not stopping me functioning, exactly, but it is starting to get in the way. Is that a fair way to put it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It hasn’t stopped me doing my job or anything like that, but it has started to affect things with people at work and in my community.\n\nIn meetings, and at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I go home feeling embarrassed rather than feeling like I’ve connected with them. I suppose that does make it harder to maintain those relationships comfortably. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: A bit distressed, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound as if I’m falling apart. It’s more that I feel upset afterward, especially when I can tell people have lost patience with me in meetings or at book club.\n\nI leave feeling embarrassed, like I’ve made things harder or awkward, instead of feeling connected to people. So yes, it does weigh on me somewhat. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life, such as difficulties with work or relationships, that you think might be related to these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… not hugely, but enough that I’ve noticed it. It’s mostly with relationships at work and in the community.\n\nIn meetings, or at book club, people sometimes seem to lose patience with me, and then I come away feeling embarrassed, like I’ve not handled myself very well. Instead of feeling connected to people, I end up worrying I’ve annoyed them. So yes, I suppose that is affecting my day-to-day life a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining focus during tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a major problem with focus, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, I can usually get through tasks, especially if they’re important. I suppose everyone’s mind wanders sometimes, doesn’t it? But I’m not sure I’d describe it as often having trouble sustaining focus. Does that answer it properly?\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I probably was, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was a terribly naughty child or anything.\n\nI was considered bright, but my report cards did say things like I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. And my mother used to joke that before I was ten, I’d worn a little path between my desk and the pencil sharpener because I was up and down so much.\n\nSo yes, compared with other children, I suppose I was more restless. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or participate in social activities because of your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve been completely unable to work or socialise, no… that sounds a bit stronger than it feels.\n\nBut it has started to get in the way a little. At work, in meetings, and in things like book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then afterwards I feel quite embarrassed. I go home thinking, “Oh dear, I’ve done it again,” instead of feeling like I’ve enjoyed being with people.\n\nSo I’m still doing those things, but it does affect how comfortable I feel in them, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and impulsive behaviors for most of your life, including when you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though I suppose I’ve always thought of it as just being “lively” rather than a problem.\n\nEven when I was a girl, my school reports said I was bright, but that I couldn’t stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother used to joke that before I was ten I’d worn a little path between my desk and the pencil sharpener because I was up and down so much.\n\nSo yes, it does seem to go back a long way. Is that the kind of history you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job or end a relationship because of your restlessness or impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever had to actually quit a job or end a relationship because of it. That sounds rather dramatic, and I don’t think it’s gone that far.\n\nIt’s more that it can strain things a bit. At work, in meetings, and in my book club, I can sense people getting a little impatient with me sometimes, and then I leave feeling embarrassed rather than feeling connected to them. But I’m still there, still doing those things… if that answers the question properly.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose “significantly” makes me hesitate a bit, because I’m still managing day to day. I’m going to work, I’m seeing people, I’m doing my usual things.\n\nBut it has affected things, yes. Mostly in how I get on with people at work and in my community. In meetings, or at book club, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me, and then I come away feeling embarrassed rather than connected to them.\n\nSo perhaps it’s not stopping me functioning, exactly, but it is starting to get in the way. Is that a fair way to put it?"
